// Article-specific bespoke detection — Snyk Log Sniffer: AI-Powered Audit Log Insights for Security Leaders
// Hunts the actual binaries / paths / commandline fragments named

          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

SHA1-Hulud, npm supply chain incident

Snyk
2025-11-24 11 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

Snyk identified a new supply chain attack in the npm ecosystem, referred to as SHA1-Hulud. We believe this is a second wave of the Shai-Hulud attack. Learn what this attack is and how Snyk is responding.

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1204.002

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution

Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] SHA1-Hulud worm payload execution via npm preinstall (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) · [LLM] Malicious '.github/workflows/discussion.yaml' workflow file created by npm/node · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match

Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] TruffleHog secret-scanner execution on developer / CI host (SHA1-Hulud credential harvest) · [LLM] Cloud metadata service (IMDS) access from npm / node child process · [LLM] SHA1-Hulud wiper: mass deletion of user home directory by npm/node descendant
SHA1-Hulud worm payload execution via npm preinstall (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) AIInstallation High
Hunts the SHA1-Hulud worm's signature preinstall payload — node spawned by npm during package install executing setup_bun.js or bun_environment.js. This is the foothold the worm needs before pivoting to credential theft and GitHub Actions self-hosted runner registration. Rationale: setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js are the canonical SHA1-Hulud worm payload filenames named in Wiz, Datadog, Trend Micro and Palo Alto Unit 42 write-ups; pairing them with npm/node parent process is high-fidelity supply-chain compromise signal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/k/shai-hulud-npm-worm.html
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007 T1546.016
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal security team replaying IOC samples in a sandbox for IR validation
  • Red team / purple team exercises simulating supply chain compromise
  • Researcher analyzing the trojanized package tarball offline
  • Developer machine restoring a quarantined node_modules tree without removing the malicious files first
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dry-run","--ignore-scripts") or FolderPath has_any ("\\sandbox\\","\\malware-lab\\","\\detonation\\"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Capture full process tree (npm/node parent, all children), SHA256 of setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js, the package.json + lockfile of the project that triggered install, and timestamps. Cross-reference the installed package name + version against Snyk's affected-packages list. Step 2 (Contain): Isolate the host immediately (Defender Live Response, Falcon RTR network containment). Rotate every credential ever stored on that host — GitHub PATs, npm tokens, AWS access keys, Azure CLI tokens, GCP application-default credentials, SSH keys. Audit GitHub org for new repos containing 'Shai-Hulud: The Second Coming' descriptions and revoke any self-hosted runner tokens. Step 3 (Hunt): Pivot on AccountName + DeviceName for outbound traffic to webhook.site, trufflehog execution, .github/workflows/discussion.yaml writes, and IMDS metadata access across the last 30 days.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("npm.exe","node.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","npm","node","yarn","pnpm")
         or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install","preinstall"))
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("npm","node","yarn","pnpm")
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install","preinstall")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: SHA1-Hulud npm Worm Preinstall Payload Execution
id: 9d3a5b1c-4e2a-4f6a-9c1e-7b3a1e9d4f5a
description: Detects the SHA1-Hulud npm supply chain worm's preinstall payload (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) spawned from npm/node lifecycle hooks.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/sha1-hulud-npm-supply-chain-incident/
  - https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\npm.exe'
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\yarn.exe'
      - '\pnpm.exe'
  selection_payload:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'setup_bun.js'
      - 'bun_environment.js'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_payload
level: high
source:(windows.sysmon OR linux.auditd) (@ParentImage:(*\\npm.exe OR *\\node.exe OR *\\yarn.exe OR *\\pnpm.exe) OR @process.parent.name:(npm OR node OR yarn OR pnpm)) (@CommandLine:(*setup_bun.js* OR *bun_environment.js*) OR @process.command_line:(*setup_bun.js* OR *bun_environment.js*))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ParentBaseFileName=/^(npm|node|yarn|pnpm)(\.exe)?$/i
| regex("(setup_bun\.js|bun_environment\.js)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine, SHA256HashData], function=count())

TruffleHog secret-scanner execution on developer / CI host (SHA1-Hulud credential harvest) AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts execution of the TruffleHog binary on workstations and CI runners — the SHA1-Hulud worm bundles TruffleHog to harvest GitHub PATs, npm tokens and cloud keys from disk before exfiltration. TruffleHog is legitimate on security-team boxes but anomalous everywhere else. Rationale: TruffleHog usage by the SHA1-Hulud worm is corroborated by Wiz, Trend Micro and Datadog reports; scoping the parent to node/npm/bun/shell distinguishes worm activity from legitimate AppSec use. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.datadoghq.com/blog/shai-hulud-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/k/shai-hulud-npm-worm.html
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1555 T1083
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate AppSec / SecOps team running TruffleHog on a sanctioned scanning workstation
  • Pre-commit secret-scan hooks where TruffleHog is intentionally invoked from a developer shell
  • CI/CD pipeline jobs that explicitly run TruffleHog as a quality gate (scope detection to non-CI accounts)
  • DevSecOps tooling demos / training sessions
Suppression filter
| where AccountName !in~ ("appsec","secops","security-scanner") and DeviceName !in~ ("appsec-scan-01","secops-jump")
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Verify whether the user / host is a known AppSec scanning endpoint. If not, capture full parent chain — if parent is node/npm/bun the verdict is almost certainly malicious. Confirm by checking for setup_bun.js or bun_environment.js writes on the same host within the last 24h. Step 2 (Contain): Isolate host. Treat ALL secrets accessible from the account as compromised — rotate GitHub PATs, npm tokens, cloud keys (AWS/Azure/GCP), SSH keys, and any tokens in ~/.netrc, ~/.npmrc, ~/.aws, ~/.azure, ~/.config/gcloud, ~/.ssh. Step 3 (Hunt): Search webhook.site DNS/HTTP from the same host, look for outbound POSTs containing JSON payloads >10KB, audit GitHub org for new public repos created by the user in the last 7 days, and review GitHub Actions self-hosted runners registered recently.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName has "trufflehog"
   or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("trufflehog filesystem","trufflehog git ","trufflehog github","trufflehog --json")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","bun.exe","sh","bash","zsh","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
union isfuzzy=true
  (ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
    | where ActingProcessName has "trufflehog"
       or ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("trufflehog filesystem","trufflehog git ","trufflehog github")
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
  ),

    | where NewProcessName has "trufflehog" or CommandLine has_any ("trufflehog filesystem","trufflehog git ","trufflehog github")
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
  )

Outbound exfiltration to webhook.site from npm / node / bun process tree AICommand & Control High
Hunts the SHA1-Hulud exfiltration channel — POST requests to webhook.site originated by node/npm/bun/yarn processes. webhook.site is the worm's canonical drop point for stolen GitHub PATs and cloud credentials. Rationale: webhook.site is the named exfiltration endpoint corroborated across Wiz, Unit 42, and Datadog SHA1-Hulud reports; constraining the initiating process to npm-ecosystem binaries removes the legitimate webhook.site QA use case from the signal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.datadoghq.com/blog/shai-hulud-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1567 T1071.001 T1102
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer using webhook.site as a legitimate webhook testing endpoint from a Node.js project
  • QA engineers testing outbound webhook integrations against webhook.site sandboxes
  • Tutorial / training material walking through webhook.site as an HTTP debugging tool
  • Automated tests that POST to webhook.site for assertion purposes
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessAccountName in~ ("qa-webhook-tests","webhook-sandbox") and DeviceName has_prefix "qa-")
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Pull the full process tree of the initiating node/npm process and look for sibling setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js / trufflehog activity within ±5 minutes. Inspect the webhook.site path — random UUID slugs are normal, but inspect the POST body size (worm typically exfiltrates 10KB-1MB JSON blobs). Step 2 (Contain): Block webhook.site at perimeter for non-allowlisted users, isolate the host, rotate every credential touchable from the account. Capture memory + npm cache + node_modules of the project at install time. Step 3 (Hunt): Sweep org-wide for any other DNS to webhook.site originating from node/npm/bun in the last 30 days; correlate with GitHub audit log for new PATs created or workflows added.

| where RemoteUrl has "webhook.site" or RemoteUrl matches regex @"webhook\.site\/[a-f0-9-]{8,}"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

  ImDnsActivity

  | where DnsQuery has "webhook.site"

)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Cloud metadata service (IMDS) access from npm / node child process AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts SHA1-Hulud's cloud-credential harvest stage — node/npm-spawned processes hitting AWS 169.254.169.254 IMDS, Azure 169.254.169.254 metadata, or GCP metadata.google.internal. Legitimate apps reach IMDS, but never via a freshly-installed npm package's preinstall context. Rationale: Cloud credential harvest from AWS/Azure/GCP IMDS is the article's named escalation path and corroborated by Wiz + Unit 42; the 169.254.169.254 link-local + node-family parent is a high-signal combination because legitimate apps that hit IMDS rarely come from a fresh npm install context. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1552.005 T1078.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Cloud SDKs (aws-sdk, @azure/identity, @google-cloud/*) legitimately fetching instance credentials at app startup from a long-running service
  • CI/CD jobs running on EC2/GCE/Azure VM that legitimately use IMDS for OIDC token exchange
  • Node.js apps using node-fetch / axios to instance-metadata endpoints in serverless contexts
  • Cloud-init bootstrap scripts on freshly-provisioned dev VMs
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("@aws-sdk/credential-provider-imds","DefaultAzureCredential","google-auth-library") and isempty(MaliciousParentCmd))
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Correlate the IMDS hit with a parent process tree showing npm/node lifecycle hooks within ±10 minutes; if the same host shows setup_bun.js or webhook.site activity, the verdict is confirmed worm. Step 2 (Contain): On AWS, immediately revoke the instance role's temporary credentials (aws sts get-caller-identity → revoke the role's sessions via IAM); on Azure, revoke the managed identity; on GCP, rotate the service account key. Snapshot and isolate the instance/host. Step 3 (Hunt): Pull CloudTrail/Azure Activity/GCP audit logs for any API calls made by the harvested principal within the previous 24h — look for unusual GetSecretValue, ListAccessKeys, AssumeRole, or storage-bucket downloads.

Malicious '.github/workflows/discussion.yaml' workflow file created by npm/node AIInstallation High
Detects SHA1-Hulud's GitHub Actions persistence: the worm writes a discussion.yaml workflow file under .github/workflows/ inside cloned/checked-out repositories to inject self-hosted runner registration and exfiltration steps that propagate to downstream pipelines. Rationale: discussion.yaml workflow file injection is the documented SHA1-Hulud GitHub Actions persistence mechanism (Wiz, Datadog); requiring an npm/node parent removes legitimate developer commits adding a workflow named discussion. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.datadoghq.com/blog/shai-hulud-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1547 T1053.005 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A developer intentionally writing a GitHub Discussion bot workflow named discussion.yaml as part of normal dev workflow (but rarely written by node/npm)
  • Yeoman / Plop / cookiecutter scaffolding tools that template workflow files on `npm init`
  • Renovate/Dependabot generating workflow updates via a node CLI
  • An IDE extension that runs under node and creates workflow files at user request
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("yeoman","plop","renovate","dependabot") and not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")))
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Read the contents of the discussion.yaml file — confirm it contains `runs-on: self-hosted`, references to setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js, or outbound POST steps to webhook.site. Identify the parent repo. Step 2 (Contain): Delete the file, revert the commit, isolate the host. Audit GitHub org for every PR/branch that introduced a discussion.yaml in the last 30 days. Revoke GitHub App installation tokens used by self-hosted runners and de-register suspicious runners. Step 3 (Hunt): Cross-reference all repos with a recent discussion.yaml commit against the affected-npm-packages list; look for `Shai-Hulud: The Second Coming` repository descriptions across the org's public repos and forks.

SHA1-Hulud wiper: mass deletion of user home directory by npm/node descendant AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the SHA1-Hulud destructive-fallback wiper — when credential harvest fails, the worm deletes the developer's home directory. Detects high-volume file delete events under %USERPROFILE% / /home/* whose initiating process traces back to node/npm/bun. Rationale: The wiper / home-directory-deletion destructive fallback is explicitly named in the article's Nov 24 3:30pm update and corroborated by Wiz and Unit 42; a node/npm/bun-descended process performing >200 deletes under a user home in 5 minutes is anomalous outside `rm -rf node_modules` (which doesn't recurse through ~ ). Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1485 T1561.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer running `rm -rf ~/node_modules` or `rm -rf ~/.npm/_cacache` to clean caches (high count, but constrained directories)
  • CI cleanup steps that remove the workspace at end of pipeline (scope to non-CI runners)
  • Containerized dev environments / Devcontainers tearing down a workspace
  • User running `npm cache clean --force` or `bun pm cache rm`
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("rm -rf node_modules","npm cache clean","bun pm cache rm","yarn cache clean") and DistinctTopLevelDirs < 3)
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Inspect the SamplePaths set — wiper hits broad subtrees (~/.ssh, ~/.aws, ~/Documents, ~/.gnupg) whereas cache cleans stay inside ~/.npm or ~/.cache. If broad → confirmed wiper. Check for prior setup_bun.js / TruffleHog / webhook.site activity on same host within the last 24h. Step 2 (Contain): Immediately isolate the host. DO NOT reboot — capture memory and disk image first; some wiped files may still be recoverable from cache/journal. Block the user's account from VPN, GitHub, npm, cloud consoles until rotation completes. Step 3 (Hunt): Pivot org-wide for similar delete bursts under /home / C:\\Users in the previous 7 days; correlate with the affected-npm-packages list to find other infected developers; engage IR for forensic recovery of stolen secrets that may have already been exfiltrated before wipe.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "FileDeleted"
| where FolderPath startswith @"C:\Users\" or FolderPath startswith "/home/" or FolderPath startswith "/Users/" or FolderPath startswith "/root/"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","bun.exe","node","npm","bun","rm","sh","bash","zsh","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| summarize DeleteCount = count(),
            SamplePaths = make_set(FolderPath, 10),
            FirstDelete = min(Timestamp),
            LastDelete = max(Timestamp),
            DistinctTopLevelDirs = dcount(tostring(split(FolderPath, @"\")[2]))
            by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, bin(Timestamp, 5m)
| where DeleteCount > 200
| order by FirstDelete desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType == "FileDeleted"
| where TargetFilePath startswith @"C:\Users\" or TargetFilePath startswith "/home/" or TargetFilePath startswith "/Users/" or TargetFilePath startswith "/root/"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node","npm","bun","rm","sh","bash","zsh","powershell","pwsh")
| summarize DeleteCount = count(),
            FirstDelete = min(TimeGenerated),
            LastDelete = max(TimeGenerated)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| where DeleteCount > 200
| order by FirstDelete desc
(source:windows.sysmon OR source:linux.auditd) @EventID:(23 OR 26) (@TargetFilename:(C:\\Users\\* OR /home/* OR /Users/* OR /root/*)) (@Image:(*\\node.exe OR *\\npm.exe OR *\\bun.exe OR *\\rm OR *\\sh OR *\\bash OR *\\powershell.exe OR *\\pwsh.exe) OR @process.executable.path:(*/node OR */npm OR */bun OR */rm OR */sh OR */bash))
#event_simpleName=FileDeleted
| regex("^(C:\\\\Users\\\\|/home/|/Users/|/root/)", field=TargetFileName)
| ContextImageFileName=/(node|npm|bun|rm|sh|bash|zsh|powershell|pwsh)(\.exe)?$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, UserName], function=count(), limit=max)
| _count > 200

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) Exploitation High
Browser-pasted PowerShell launched from explorer/Run dialog — ClickFix social engineering.
ATT&CKT1204.004 T1059.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\explorer.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\RuntimeBroker.exe"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09", "62ee164b9b306250c1172583f138c9614139264f889fa99614903c12755468d0", "f099c5d9ec417d4445a0328ac0ada9cde79fc37410914103ae9c609cbc0ee068", "cbb9bc5a8496243e02f3cc080efbe3e4a1430ba0671f2e43a202bf45b05479cd", "a3894003ad1d293ba96d77881ccd2071446dc3f65f434669b49b3da92421901a", "b74caeaa75e077c99f7d44f46daaf9796a3be43ecf24f2a1fd381844669da777", "dc67467a39b70d1cd4c1f7f7a459b35058163592f4a9e8fb4dffcbba98ef210c", "4b2399646573bb737c4969563303d8ee2e9ddbd1b271f1ca9e35ea78062538db", "d60ec97eea19fffb4809bc35b91033b52490ca11", "3d7570d14d34b0ba137d502f042b27b0f37a59fa", "d1829b4708126dcc7bea7437c04d1f10eacd4a16"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-61757")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-61757")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender

Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] LittleDaemon / DaemonicLogistics update-hijack URL pattern (popup_4.2.0.2246.dll, /update/updateInfo.bzp, /update/file6.bdat, /update/file2.
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — PlushDaemon compromises network devices for adversary-in-the-middle attacks
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] DaemonicLogistics fake-Tencent payload drop (logo.gif at %PROGRAMDATA%\Tencent\QQUpdateMgr\UpdateFiles)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] PlushDaemon EdgeStepper hijacking infrastructure (wcsset.com / 47.242.198.250 / 8.212.132.120) contact · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
PlushDaemon EdgeStepper hijacking infrastructure (wcsset.com / 47.242.198.250 / 8.212.132.120) contact AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts for any host or perimeter device that resolves or connects to PlushDaemon's DNS/hijacking nodes on the wcsset[.]com domain or the two Alibaba-hosted IPs ESET attributed to the campaign. A hit means either a Windows victim is being update-hijacked, or a router/network appliance has been compromised and is forwarding DNS to the malicious node. Rationale: All three indicators (47.242.198.250, 8.212.132.120, *.wcsset.com) are confirmed in the ESET malware-ioc GitHub repo and republished by The Hacker News, BleepingComputer and Help Net Security. The wcsset.com SLD is registered solely for this PlushDaemon infrastructure, so any contact is high-fidelity. Using DNS+netflow+HTTP across both Defender and Sentinel ASIM/CEF surfaces catches both the Windows victims and any compromised router still proxying. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/eset/malware-ioc/tree/master/PlushDaemon • https://thehackernews.com/2025/11/edgestepper-implant-reroutes-dns.html • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/plushdaemon-hackers-hijack-software-updates-in-supply-chain-attacks/ • https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2025/11/19/eset-plushdaemon-dns-hijacking/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1568.002 T1659 T1583.002

  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where RemoteIP in (plushIPs) or RemoteUrl has plushDomain
  | project Timestamp, Source="DeviceNetworkEvents", DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort, ActionType ),
( DeviceEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where ActionType == "DnsQueryResponse"
  | extend QueryName = tostring(parse_json(AdditionalFields).QueryName)
  | where QueryName has plushDomain
  | project Timestamp, Source="DeviceEvents-DnsQueryResponse", DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP="", RemoteUrl=QueryName, RemotePort=int(null), ActionType )
| order by Timestamp desc

LittleDaemon / DaemonicLogistics update-hijack URL pattern (popup_4.2.0.2246.dll, /update/updateInfo.bzp, /update/file6.bdat, /update/file2. AIDelivery High
Hunts the very specific HTTP request paths LittleDaemon and DaemonicLogistics use to call back to the hijacking node — including the LittleDaemon DLL filename, the updateInfo.bzp probe, the bdat staging files, and the hardcoded `/update/latest/new_version?tp=1&g=15&c=0` query string. These paths only exist in this campaign and will not be issued by genuine Sogou / Baidu / Tencent updaters. Rationale: The URL fragments are pulled directly from the ESET narrative analysis: popup_4.2.0.2246.dll is the LittleDaemon DLL filename, /update/updateInfo.bzp is the LittleDaemon resource path, /update/file6.bdat and /update/file2.bdat are the SlowStepper payload requests, and the tp=1&g=15&c=0 form is hardcoded into DaemonicLogistics. None of these strings appear in legitimate Sogou / Baidu / Tencent updater traffic. The same paths are republished in the ESET malware-ioc repo and summarised by The Hacker News. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/eset/malware-ioc/tree/master/PlushDaemon • https://thehackernews.com/2025/11/edgestepper-implant-reroutes-dns.html • https://gbhackers.com/plushdaemon-hackers/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1105 T1659 T1036.005
Data sourcesWeb.Web
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where isnotempty(RemoteUrl)
| where RemoteUrl has_any (
      "/update/updateInfo.bzp",
      "/update/file6.bdat",
      "/update/file2.bdat",
      "popup_4.2.0.2246.dll")
   or (RemoteUrl has "/update/latest/new_version"
       and (RemoteUrl has "tp=1&g=15&c=0"
            or RemoteUrl matches regex @"(?i)tp=2&c=0&s=\d+&mac="))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessSHA256, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort, ActionType
| order by Timestamp desc

         and (RequestURL has "tp=1&g=15&c=0"

         and (Url has "tp=1&g=15&c=0"

title: PlushDaemon LittleDaemon and DaemonicLogistics Update-Hijack URL Pattern

status: experimental
description: Detects HTTP requests to URL paths unique to PlushDaemon's LittleDaemon downloader and DaemonicLogistics second-stage during the hijacked Sogou Pinyin / Baidu / Tencent QQ / WPS Office update flow. The popup_4.2.0.2246.dll filename, /update/updateInfo.bzp probe, /update/file6.bdat and /update/file2.bdat staging files, and the hardcoded /update/latest/new_version?tp=1&g=15&c=0 callback are not used by any legitimate updater.

  condition: selection_paths or selection_query_combo
falsepositives:

DaemonicLogistics fake-Tencent payload drop (logo.gif at %PROGRAMDATA%\Tencent\QQUpdateMgr\UpdateFiles) AIInstallation High
Hunts the highly specific file artifact DaemonicLogistics writes after fetching the first SlowStepper stage from the hijacking node: a ZIP-masqueraded-as-GIF (PK\x03\x04 magic) saved as logo.gif inside %PROGRAMDATA%\Tencent\QQUpdateMgr\UpdateFiles. Pairs the path/filename with the published LittleDaemon SHA-1 hashes for direct attribution. Rationale: ESET explicitly states DaemonicLogistics writes its first-magic-tagged payload to %PROGRAMDATA%\Tencent\QQUpdateMgr\UpdateFiles\logo.gif and creates a Tencent subdirectory for masquerading (T1036.005). The path+filename combination is unique to this campaign. The query also pivots on the four published SHA-1 hashes (LittleDaemon DLL/EXE and EdgeStepper bioset/bioset.conf) which are mirrored in the ESET malware-ioc GitHub repo, giving direct attribution if the file artifact has already been cleaned but the binary executed. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/eset/malware-ioc/tree/master/PlushDaemon • https://thehackernews.com/2025/11/edgestepper-implant-reroutes-dns.html • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/plushdaemon-hackers-hijack-software-updates-in-supply-chain-attacks/
ATT&CKT1036.005 T1036.008 T1105 T1106
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
let LittleDaemonSHA1 = dynamic(["69974455D8C13C5D57C1EE91E147FF9AED49AEBC","2857BC730952682D39F426D185769938E839A125"]);
let EdgeStepperSHA1 = dynamic(["8F569641691ECB3888CD4C11932A5B8E13F04B07","06177810D61A69F34091CC9689B813740D4C260F"]);
union isfuzzy=true
( DeviceFileEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
  | where FolderPath has @"\Tencent\QQUpdateMgr\UpdateFiles"
      and FileName =~ "logo.gif"
  | project Timestamp, Signal="DaemonicLogistics_logo.gif_drop", DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName ),
( DeviceFileEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where SHA1 in (LittleDaemonSHA1, EdgeStepperSHA1)
  | project Timestamp, Signal="PlushDaemon_known_hash", DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName ),
( DeviceProcessEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where SHA1 in (LittleDaemonSHA1) or InitiatingProcessSHA1 in (LittleDaemonSHA1)
  | project Timestamp, Signal="LittleDaemon_exec", DeviceName, FileName=FileName, FolderPath, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName=AccountName ),
( DeviceImageLoadEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where SHA1 in (LittleDaemonSHA1)
  | project Timestamp, Signal="LittleDaemon_dll_load", DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName )
| order by Timestamp desc
let LittleDaemonSHA1 = dynamic(["69974455D8C13C5D57C1EE91E147FF9AED49AEBC","2857BC730952682D39F426D185769938E839A125"]);
let EdgeStepperSHA1 = dynamic(["8F569641691ECB3888CD4C11932A5B8E13F04B07","06177810D61A69F34091CC9689B813740D4C260F"]);

( ImFileEvent

      and TargetFileName =~ "logo.gif"

( ImFileEvent
  | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
  | where TargetFileSHA1 in (LittleDaemonSHA1, EdgeStepperSHA1)
  | project TimeGenerated, Signal="PlushDaemon_known_hash", DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA1, TargetFileSHA256, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername ),
( SecurityEvent
  | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
  | where EventID == 4688
  | where FileHash has_any (LittleDaemonSHA1)
  | project TimeGenerated, Signal="LittleDaemon_4688", DvcHostname=Computer, TargetFilePath=NewProcessName, TargetFileName="", TargetFileSHA1=tostring(FileHash), TargetFileSHA256="", ActingProcessName=ParentProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine=CommandLine, ActorUsername=Account )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Detects DaemonicLogistics writing the masqueraded ZIP-as-GIF first-stage SlowStepper payload (PK\x03\x04\x0A\x1B\x2C\x3D magic) to %PROGRAMDATA%\Tencent\QQUpdateMgr\UpdateFiles\logo.gif. Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High
Encoded or obfuscated PowerShell — common across loaders, recon, and post-exploitation.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Obfuscated / Encoded PowerShell Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d08
status: experimental
description: |
    PowerShell launched with -EncodedCommand, base64 strings, hidden
    window, no-profile, bypass policy, IEX / Invoke-Expression / Net
    .WebClient.DownloadString — combinations indicating evasion.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1027

    - attack.defense-evasion

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

    selection_image:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'

        CommandLine|contains:
            - '-enc '
            - 'EncodedCommand'
            - 'FromBase64String'
            - 'Invoke-Expression'
            - 'IEX('
            - 'DownloadString'
            - 'Net.WebClient'
            - '-w hidden'

            - '-nop'
            - '-NoProfile'
            - '-ep bypass'
            - '-ExecutionPolicy Bypass'

      cross-shell escaping

        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium

| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine

// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent

| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"

          NewProcessName, CommandLine

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Automated Package-Publication Incident IndonesianFoods in the NPM Ecosystem Linked to Crypto Reward-Farming Scam

Snyk
2025-11-13 5 use cases 2 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

In November 2025, a large-scale surge of package publications on the NPM registry with similar structures and naming patterns was discovered. Understand the details of the incident.

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
Detected
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] IndonesianFoods npm spam package install on developer/CI endpoint
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Automated Package-Publication Incident IndonesianFoods in the NPM Ecosystem Link
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] IndonesianFoods auto-publish artifact (auto.js / publishScript.js) dropped in node_modules
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] TEA Protocol (tea.xyz) DNS resolution from developer or build endpoint
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
IndonesianFoods npm spam package install on developer/CI endpoint AIDelivery High
Hunts for npm/yarn/pnpm install commands referencing the IndonesianFoods TEA Protocol reward-farming spam packages — base names vointea/voinzaril and the suffix families -wekto/-riris/-z3n/-kyuki/-breki/-notthedevs, plus the high-download outlier ksni-technical-test. Catches developers or build agents pulling any of the ~4,000–5,000 derivative spam packages that the auto-publication script seeded onto the registry. Rationale: Pivots on the SPECIFIC base package names (vointea, voinzaril) and suffix families (-wekto, -riris, -z3n) called out by Snyk, augmented with the -kyuki/-breki/-notthedevs suffixes and the ksni-technical-test outlier confirmed in Endor Labs and The Hacker News deep-dives. Any one match against an npm install command line is article-specific and has no benign reading. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/automated-package-publication-incident-indonesianfoods/ • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn (IndonesianFoods deep-dive — verified via memory observations 1327/1328) • https://thehackernews.com/ (IndonesianFoods coverage — verified via memory observations 1327/1328)
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher reproducing the incident in a contained VM (matches exactly, treat as authorised)
  • Threat-intel analyst pulling sample tarballs into a sandbox via `npm pack`/`npm install --no-save`
  • Misnamed but legitimate internal package that coincidentally contains one of the substring tokens (very unlikely with the suffix patterns)
  • Dependency-confusion scanner crawling registry surface and pulling samples
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !in~ ("sandbox-svc","malware-research") and DeviceName !startswith "DETONATE-"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the package name in the command line is one of the IndonesianFoods families; pull the device's lockfile (package-lock.json / yarn.lock) and SBOM to see whether the package landed transitively or was direct. 2) Contain: remove the package from the project, purge node_modules and the lockfile entry, re-install from a clean tree, and snapshot the dropped node_modules folder for inspection (the package itself is low-risk but its presence in CI artifacts pollutes downstream SBOMs). 3) Hunt: pivot on the device's last 30 days of npm/yarn/pnpm commands for other IndonesianFoods families, query DeviceFileEvents for auto.js / publishScript.js artifacts under node_modules, and check whether any internal package was republished from this host while the spam dependency was present.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("npm.exe","node.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","npx.exe")
    or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("npm.exe","node.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","npx.exe")
| extend FullCmd = strcat(tostring(ProcessCommandLine), " || ", tostring(InitiatingProcessCommandLine))
| where FullCmd has_any ("install"," add "," i ")
| where FullCmd matches regex @"(?i)(vointea|voinzaril|-(wekto|riris|z3n|kyuki|breki|notthedevs)|ksni-technical-test)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where (NewProcessName has_any ("\\npm.exe","\\node.exe","\\yarn.exe","\\pnpm.exe","\\npx.exe")
      or ParentProcessName has_any ("\\npm.exe","\\yarn.exe","\\pnpm.exe","\\npx.exe"))
| where CommandLine has_any ("install"," add "," i ")
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(vointea|voinzaril|-(wekto|riris|z3n|kyuki|breki|notthedevs)|ksni-technical-test)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, ParentProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: IndonesianFoods npm spam package install
id: e7c4a2f8-3b91-4d6c-9a7e-1f2d8e5b3c4a
description: Detects npm/yarn/pnpm install commands that reference the IndonesianFoods TEA Protocol reward-farming spam package families (vointea/voinzaril base names and -wekto/-riris/-z3n/-kyuki/-breki/-notthedevs suffix variants).
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/automated-package-publication-incident-indonesianfoods/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1059.007
  - attack.initial_access
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_proc:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\npm.exe'
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\yarn.exe'
      - '\pnpm.exe'
      - '\npx.exe'
  selection_verb:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - ' install '
      - ' add '
      - ' i '
  selection_pkg:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'vointea'
      - 'voinzaril'
      - '-wekto'
      - '-riris'
      - '-z3n'
      - '-kyuki'
      - '-breki'
      - '-notthedevs'
      - 'ksni-technical-test'
  condition: selection_proc and selection_verb and selection_pkg
falsepositives:
  - Security researchers reproducing the incident in sandboxes
  - Threat-intel teams pulling sample packages for triage
level: medium
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 (@Image:(*\\npm.exe OR *\\node.exe OR *\\yarn.exe OR *\\pnpm.exe OR *\\npx.exe) OR @ParentImage:(*\\npm.exe OR *\\yarn.exe OR *\\pnpm.exe OR *\\npx.exe)) @CommandLine:(*install* OR *" add "* OR *" i "*) @CommandLine:(*vointea* OR *voinzaril* OR *-wekto* OR *-riris* OR *-z3n* OR *-kyuki* OR *-breki* OR *-notthedevs* OR *ksni-technical-test*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ImageFileName=/(npm|node|yarn|pnpm|npx)\.exe$/i OR ParentBaseFileName=/(npm|yarn|pnpm|npx)\.exe$/i
| regex("(install|\\sadd\\s|\\si\\s)", field=CommandLine, flags="i")
| regex("(vointea|voinzaril|-(wekto|riris|z3n|kyuki|breki|notthedevs)|ksni-technical-test)", field=CommandLine, flags="i")
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

IndonesianFoods auto-publish artifact (auto.js / publishScript.js) dropped in node_modules AIInstallation High
Detects the IndonesianFoods campaign's auto-publication scripts — auto.js or publishScript.js — being written into a node_modules subfolder whose package name matches the vointea/voinzaril/-wekto/-riris/-z3n/-kyuki/-breki/-notthedevs naming families. Per Snyk and Endor Labs these scripts are the ONLY 'malicious' functionality in the campaign: they re-template the package and republish under a new name to farm tea.xyz rewards. Rationale: Pivots on the article's named automation artifacts (auto.js, publishScript.js — described by Snyk as the publication automation that lacks an exit condition) constrained to node_modules subfolders whose package name matches a confirmed IndonesianFoods family. Both axes have to match, so unrelated repos containing files named auto.js won't fire — only the campaign's actual artifact will. Endor Labs and Hacker News both confirm these scripts as the campaign's core mechanism. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/automated-package-publication-incident-indonesianfoods/ • Endor Labs IndonesianFoods analysis (verified via memory observations 1327/1328) • The Hacker News IndonesianFoods coverage (verified via memory observations 1327/1328)
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007 T1496
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher detonating one of the spam packages in a contained VM
  • SBOM-scanning tool unpacking package tarballs into a temp node_modules tree for inspection
  • Mirror/proxy registry caching the package locally for later analysis
  • Legacy benign package called publish.js inside a tea.xyz-themed dev project (vanishingly rare)
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !startswith "DETONATE-" and DeviceName !startswith "SANDBOX-" and InitiatingProcessAccountName !in~ ("malware-research","sbom-scanner")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the file path contains BOTH a campaign-family package name AND the auto-publish script name; pull the file hash and compare against any seen previously in the org. 2) Contain: quarantine the file, remove the parent package from the project, and block the package name (and family suffix) in the org's npm proxy / Snyk Advisor policy so future installs are gated. 3) Hunt: pivot on InitiatingProcessAccountName for any subsequent `npm publish` / `npm pack` activity from the same user (would indicate the publish script was actually executed, not just unpacked) and search DeviceNetworkEvents for tea.xyz resolutions from the host across the last 30 days.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in~ ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has @"\node_modules\"
| where FileName in~ ("auto.js","publishScript.js","publish.js")
| where FolderPath matches regex @"(?i)\\node_modules\\[^\\]*(vointea|voinzaril|-(wekto|riris|z3n|kyuki|breki|notthedevs))[^\\]*\\"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where TargetFilePath has @"\node_modules\"
| where TargetFileName in~ ("auto.js","publishScript.js","publish.js")
| where TargetFilePath matches regex @"(?i)\\node_modules\\[^\\]*(vointea|voinzaril|-(wekto|riris|z3n|kyuki|breki|notthedevs))[^\\]*\\"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc
